					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">10th and 11th-century Grand Prince of Kiev and Novgorod</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Prince Vladimir" redirects here. For the 2006 Russian film, see <a href="/wiki/Prince_Vladimir_(film)" title="Prince Vladimir (film)">Prince Vladimir (film)</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">In this name that follows <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Slavic_naming_customs" title="Eastern Slavic naming customs">Eastern Slavic naming conventions</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Patronymic" title="Patronymic">patronymic</a> is <i> Sviatoslavich</i>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .infobox-3cols-child{margin:auto}.mw-parser-output .infobox .navbar{font-size:100%}body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-header,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-subheader,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-above,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-title,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-image,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-full-data,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-below{text-align:center}</style><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above fn" style="background-color: #cbe; font-size: 125%">Vladimir the Great</th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image photo"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Coin_of_Vladimir_the_Great.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Coin_of_Vladimir_the_Great.JPG/220px-Coin_of_Vladimir_the_Great.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="213" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/11/Coin_of_Vladimir_the_Great.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="328" data-file-height="317" /></a></span><div class="infobox-caption" style="line-height:normal;padding-bottom:0.2em;padding-top:0.2em;">Vladimir's effigy on one of <a href="/wiki/Money_of_Kievan_Rus%27" title="Money of Kievan Rus&#39;">his coins</a>. He is crowned in the Byzantine style, holding a cross-mounted staff in one hand and a Khazar-inspired trident<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> in the other.</div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color: #e4dcf6;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em 0.2em"><a href="/wiki/Grand_Prince_of_Kiev" title="Grand Prince of Kiev">Grand Prince of Kiev</a></th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Reign</th><td class="infobox-data">11 June 978&#160;&#8211;&#32;15 July 1015</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Predecessor</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Yaropolk_I_of_Kiev" title="Yaropolk I of Kiev">Yaropolk I</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Successor</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Sviatopolk_I_of_Kiev" title="Sviatopolk I of Kiev">Sviatopolk I</a></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color: #e4dcf6;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em 0.2em"><a href="/wiki/Prince_of_Novgorod" title="Prince of Novgorod">Prince of Novgorod</a></th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Reign</th><td class="infobox-data">970&#160;&#8211;&#32;<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;988</span></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Predecessor</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Sviatoslav_I_of_Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Sviatoslav I of Kiev">Sviatoslav I</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Successor</th><td class="infobox-data">Vysheslav</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color: #e4dcf6;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em 0.2em"><div style="height: 4px; <p><b>Vladimir I Sviatoslavich</b> or <b>Volodymyr I Sviatoslavych</b><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/Old_East_Slavic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Old East Slavic language">Old East Slavic</a>: <span lang="orv">Володимѣръ Свѧтославичь</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Old East Slavic-language romanization"><i lang="orv-Latn">Volodiměr Svętoslavič</i></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Christian_name" title="Christian name">Christian name</a>: <i>Basil</i>;<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;958</span>&#160;&#8211;&#32;15 July 1015), nicknamed <b>the Great</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> was <a href="/wiki/Prince_of_Novgorod" title="Prince of Novgorod">Prince of Novgorod</a> from 970 and <a href="/wiki/Grand_Prince_of_Kiev" title="Grand Prince of Kiev">Grand Prince of Kiev</a> from 978 until his death in 1015.<sup id="cite_ref-Feldbrugge_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Feldbrugge-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hanak_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hanak-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonised</a> him as <b>Saint Vladimir</b>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Vladimir's father was <a href="/wiki/Sviatoslav_I" title="Sviatoslav I">Sviatoslav I</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Rurik_dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Rurik dynasty">Rurik dynasty</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-britannica.com_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-britannica.com-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> After the death of his father in 972, Vladimir, who was then the prince of <a href="/wiki/Veliky_Novgorod" title="Veliky Novgorod">Novgorod</a>, was forced to flee abroad after his brother <a href="/wiki/Yaropolk_I_of_Kiev" title="Yaropolk I of Kiev">Yaropolk</a> murdered his other brother <a href="/wiki/Oleg_of_Drelinia" title="Oleg of Drelinia">Oleg</a> in 977 to become the sole ruler of Rus'. Vladimir assembled a <a href="/wiki/Varangian" class="mw-redirect" title="Varangian">Varangian</a> army and returned to depose Yaropolk in 978.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> By 980,<sup id="cite_ref-Hanak_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hanak-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Vladimir had consolidated his realm to the <a href="/wiki/Baltic_Sea" title="Baltic Sea">Baltic Sea</a> and solidified the frontiers against incursions of <a href="/wiki/Bulgarians" title="Bulgarians">Bulgarians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Baltic_tribes" class="mw-redirect" title="Baltic tribes">Baltic tribes</a> and Eastern nomads. Originally a follower of <a href="/wiki/Slavic_paganism" title="Slavic paganism">Slavic paganism</a>, Vladimir converted to <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> in 988,<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Christianization_of_Kievan_Rus%27" title="Christianization of Kievan Rus&#39;">Christianized the Kievan Rus'</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-britannica.com_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-britannica.com-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Name">Name</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Name">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Several scholars refer to Vladimir as <i>Volodimer</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFranklin19913_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFranklin19913-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOstrowski2006568_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOstrowski2006568-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalperin202215_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalperin202215-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> also spelled <i>Volodimir</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOstrowski201833_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOstrowski201833-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> and his descendants as <i>Volodimerovichi</i> (sometimes in lieu of "<a href="/wiki/Rurikids" title="Rurikids">Rurikids</a>").<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERaffensperger20169_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERaffensperger20169-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalperin2022viii_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalperin2022viii-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/History_of_Scandinavia" title="History of Scandinavia">history of Scandinavia</a>, Vladimir is also known as <i>Valdemar</i> or the <a href="/wiki/Old_Norse" title="Old Norse">Old Norse</a> form <i>Valdamarr</i> (see <a href="/wiki/Waldemar" title="Waldemar">Waldemar</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Rise_to_power">Rise to power</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Rise to power">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Born in 958, Vladimir was the <a href="/wiki/Illegitimate" class="mw-redirect" title="Illegitimate">illegitimate</a> and youngest son of <a href="/wiki/Sviatoslav_I_of_Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Sviatoslav I of Kiev">Sviatoslav I of Kiev</a> by his housekeeper <a href="/wiki/Malusha" title="Malusha">Malusha</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBain1911_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain1911-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Malusha is described in the <a href="/wiki/Norse_saga" class="mw-redirect" title="Norse saga">Norse sagas</a> as a prophetess who lived to the age of 100 and was brought from her cave to the palace to predict the future. Malusha's brother <a href="/wiki/Dobrynya" title="Dobrynya">Dobrynya</a> was Vladimir's tutor and most trusted advisor. Hagiographic tradition of dubious authenticity also connects his childhood with the name of his grandmother, <a href="/wiki/Olga_of_Kiev" title="Olga of Kiev">Olga of Kiev</a>, who was Christian and governed the capital during Sviatoslav's frequent military campaigns.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Transferring his capital to <a href="/wiki/Pereyaslavets" title="Pereyaslavets">Pereyaslavets</a>, Sviatoslav designated Vladimir ruler of <a href="/wiki/Novgorod_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Novgorod the Great">Novgorod the Great</a> in 970,<sup id="cite_ref-Feldbrugge_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Feldbrugge-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> but gave Kiev to his legitimate son <a href="/wiki/Yaropolk_of_Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Yaropolk of Kiev">Yaropolk</a>. After Sviatoslav's death at the hands of the <a href="/wiki/Pechenegs" title="Pechenegs">Pechenegs</a> in 972, a fratricidal war erupted in 977 between Yaropolk and his younger brother <a href="/wiki/Oleg_of_Drelinia" title="Oleg of Drelinia">Oleg</a>, ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Drevlians" title="Drevlians">Drevlians</a>; Vladimir fled abroad and assembled a Varangian army to assist him in deposing Yaropolk.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Martin_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> On his return the next year, he marched against Yaropolk. On his way to Kiev he sent ambassadors to <a href="/wiki/Rogvolod" title="Rogvolod">Rogvolod</a> (Norse: Ragnvald), prince of <a href="/wiki/Polotsk" title="Polotsk">Polotsk</a>, to sue for the hand of his daughter <a href="/wiki/Rogneda_of_Polotsk" title="Rogneda of Polotsk">Rogneda</a> (Norse: Ragnhild). The high-born princess refused to affiance herself to the son of a bondswoman (and was betrothed to Yaropolk), so Vladimir attacked Polotsk, took Ragnhild by force, and put her parents to the sword.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBain1911_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain1911-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Polotsk was a key fortress on the way to Kiev, and capturing Polotsk and <a href="/wiki/Smolensk" title="Smolensk">Smolensk</a> facilitated the taking of Kiev in 978, where he slew Yaropolk by treachery and was proclaimed <a href="/wiki/Knyaz" title="Knyaz">knyaz</a> of all <a href="/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27" title="Kievan Rus&#39;">Kievan Rus'</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hanak_16-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hanak-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Years_of_pagan_rule">Years of pagan rule</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Years of pagan rule">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Vladimir continued to expand his territories beyond his father's extensive domain. In 981, he seized the <a href="/wiki/Red_Ruthenia" title="Red Ruthenia">Cherven towns</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Polish_people" title="Polish people">Poles</a>; in 981–982, he suppressed a <a href="/wiki/Vyatichi" title="Vyatichi">Vyatichi</a> rebellion; in 983, he subdued the <a href="/wiki/Yatvingians" class="mw-redirect" title="Yatvingians">Yatvingians</a>; in 984, he conquered the <a href="/wiki/Radimichs" title="Radimichs">Radimichs</a>; and in 985, he conducted a military campaign against the <a href="/wiki/Volga_Bulgaria" title="Volga Bulgaria">Volga Bulgars</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> planting numerous fortresses and colonies on his way.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBain1911_38-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain1911-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> had spread in the region under Oleg's rule,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Vladimir had remained a thoroughgoing pagan, taking eight hundred concubines (along with numerous wives) and erecting pagan statues and shrines to gods.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He may have attempted to reform <a href="/wiki/Slavic_paganism" title="Slavic paganism">Slavic paganism</a> in an attempt to identify himself with the various gods worshipped by his subjects. He built a pagan temple on a hill in Kiev dedicated to six gods: <a href="/wiki/Perun" title="Perun">Perun</a>—the god of thunder and war, a god favored by members of the prince's druzhina (military retinue); Slavic gods <a href="/wiki/Stribog" title="Stribog">Stribog</a> and <a href="/wiki/Da%C5%BEbog" class="mw-redirect" title="Dažbog">Dazhd'bog</a>; <a href="/wiki/Mokosh" title="Mokosh">Mokosh</a>—a goddess representing Mother Nature "worshipped by Finnish tribes"; Khors and <a href="/wiki/Simargl" title="Simargl">Simargl</a>, "both of which had Iranian origins, were included, probably to appeal to the Poliane."<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Open abuse of the deities that most people in Rus' revered triggered widespread indignation. A mob killed the Christian Fyodor and his son Ioann (later, after the overall Christianisation of Kievan Rus', people came to regard these two as the first Christian martyrs in Rus', and the Orthodox Church<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> set a day to commemorate them, 25 July). Immediately after the murder of Fyodor and Ioann, early medieval Rus' saw persecutions against Christians, many of whom escaped or concealed their belief.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, Prince Vladimir mused over the incident long after, and not least for political considerations. According to the early Slavic chronicle, the <a href="/wiki/Primary_Chronicle" title="Primary Chronicle"><i>Tale of Bygone Years</i></a>, which describes life in Kievan Rus' up to the year 1110, he sent his envoys throughout the world to assess first-hand the major religions of the time: Islam, Latin Christianity, Judaism, and Byzantine Christianity<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>. They were most impressed with their visit to Constantinople, saying, "We knew not whether we were in Heaven or on Earth&#160;... We only know that God dwells there among the people, and their service is fairer than the ceremonies of other nations."<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Conversion">Conversion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Conversion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Conversion_of_Volodimer" title="Conversion of Volodimer">Conversion of Volodimer</a></div>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vasnetsov_Bapt_Vladimir.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Vasnetsov_Bapt_Vladimir.jpg/175px-Vasnetsov_Bapt_Vladimir.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="217" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Vasnetsov_Bapt_Vladimir.jpg/263px-Vasnetsov_Bapt_Vladimir.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Vasnetsov_Bapt_Vladimir.jpg/350px-Vasnetsov_Bapt_Vladimir.jpg 2x" data-file-width="945" data-file-height="1170" /></a><figcaption>The Baptism of Saint Prince Vladimir, by <a href="/wiki/Viktor_Vasnetsov" title="Viktor Vasnetsov">Viktor Vasnetsov</a> (1890)</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Primary_Chronicle" title="Primary Chronicle">Primary Chronicle</a></i> reports that in the year 986, missionaries from various peoples representing various religions arrived in Kiev, trying to convert Vladimir to their religion. In 987, after consultation with his <a href="/wiki/Boyar" title="Boyar">boyars</a>, Vladimir reportedly sent envoys to study the religions of the various neighboring peoples whose representatives had been urging him to embrace their respective faiths. Although in both stories Vladimir ultimately rejects all options except <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christianity</a>, he hesitates and does not convert.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOstrowski2006568–569_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOstrowski2006568–569-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/%D0%92%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%A5%D1%80%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B5%D0%BB%D1%8C.JPG/169px-%D0%92%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%A5%D1%80%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B5%D0%BB%D1%8C.JPG" decoding="async" width="169" height="225" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/%D0%92%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%A5%D1%80%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B5%D0%BB%D1%8C.JPG/254px-%D0%92%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%A5%D1%80%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B5%D0%BB%D1%8C.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/%D0%92%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%A5%D1%80%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B5%D0%BB%D1%8C.JPG/338px-%D0%92%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%A5%D1%80%D0%B5%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B5%D0%BB%D1%8C.JPG 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="800" /></a></span></div></div></div><div class="trow" style="display:flex"><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Monument_to_Prince_Volodymyr" title="Monument to Prince Volodymyr">Monument to Prince Volodymyr</a> on <a href="/wiki/Saint_Volodymyr_Hill" title="Saint Volodymyr Hill">Volodymyrska Hill</a> in Kyiv, near the place of the mass baptism of Kyiv people</div></div></div></div>
<p>In 988, having taken the town of <a href="/wiki/Chersonesus" title="Chersonesus">Chersonesus</a> in <a href="/wiki/Crimea" title="Crimea">Crimea</a>, he allegedly boldly negotiated for the hand of emperor <a href="/wiki/Basil_II" title="Basil II">Basil II</a>'s sister, <a href="/wiki/Anna_Porphyrogenita" title="Anna Porphyrogenita">Anna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Never before had a <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine</a> imperial princess, and one <a href="/wiki/Porphyrogenitos" class="mw-redirect" title="Porphyrogenitos">"born in the purple"</a>, married a barbarian, as matrimonial offers of French kings and <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperors</a> had been peremptorily rejected. In short, to marry the 27-year-old princess to a pagan Slav seemed impossible. Vladimir was baptized at Chersonesos, however, taking the Christian name of Basil out of compliment to his imperial brother-in-law; the sacrament was followed by his wedding to Anna.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The time period mentioned near this tag is ambiguous. (June 2022)">when?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> 
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Arab" class="mw-redirect" title="Arab">Arab</a> sources, both Muslim and Christian, present a different story of Vladimir's conversion. <a href="/wiki/Yahya_of_Antioch" title="Yahya of Antioch">Yahya of Antioch</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Al-Rudhrawari&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Al-Rudhrawari (page does not exist)">al-Rudhrawari</a>, <a href="/wiki/Al-Makin" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Makin">al-Makin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Shams_al-Din_al-Ansari_al-Dimashqi" class="mw-redirect" title="Shams al-Din al-Ansari al-Dimashqi">al-Dimashqi</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Ibn_al-Athir" title="Ibn al-Athir">ibn al-Athir</a> all give essentially the same account.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In 987, <a href="/wiki/Bardas_Sclerus" class="mw-redirect" title="Bardas Sclerus">Bardas Sclerus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bardas_Phokas_the_Younger" title="Bardas Phokas the Younger">Bardas Phocas</a> revolted against the Byzantine emperor Basil II. Both rebels briefly joined forces, but then Bardas Phocas proclaimed himself emperor on 14 September 987. Basil II turned to the Kievan Rus' for assistance, even though they were considered enemies at that time. Vladimir agreed, in exchange for a marital tie; he also agreed to accept Christianity as his religion and to Christianize his people. When the wedding arrangements were settled, Vladimir dispatched 6,000 troops to the Byzantine Empire, and they helped to put down the revolt.<sup id="cite_ref-Rus_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rus-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Christianization_of_Kievan_Rus.27"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Christianization_of_Kievan_Rus'">Christianization of Kievan Rus'</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Christianization of Kievan Rus&#039;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Christianization_of_Kievan_Rus%27" title="Christianization of Kievan Rus&#39;">Christianization of Kievan Rus'</a></div>
<p>Returning to Kiev in triumph, Vladimir <a href="/wiki/Baptism_of_Kievan_Rus%27" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptism of Kievan Rus&#39;">destroyed pagan monuments</a> and established many churches, starting with a church dedicated to <a href="/wiki/Basil_of_Caesarea" title="Basil of Caesarea">St. Basil</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Tithes" title="Church of the Tithes">Church of the Tithes</a> (989).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBain1911_38-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain1911-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 988 and 991, he baptized <a href="/wiki/Pecheneg" class="mw-redirect" title="Pecheneg">Pecheneg</a> princes <a href="/wiki/Metiga" title="Metiga">Metiga</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kuchug" title="Kuchug">Kuchug</a>, respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Christian_reign">Christian reign</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Christian reign">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Vladimir then formed a great council out of his boyars and set his <a href="/wiki/Family_life_and_children_of_Vladimir_I" title="Family life and children of Vladimir I">twelve sons</a> over his subject principalities.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBain1911_38-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain1911-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> According to the <a href="/wiki/Primary_Chronicle" title="Primary Chronicle">Primary Chronicle</a>, he founded the city of <a href="/wiki/Belgorod_Kievsky" class="mw-redirect" title="Belgorod Kievsky">Belgorod</a> in 991. In 992, he went on a campaign against the Croats, most likely the <a href="/wiki/White_Croats" title="White Croats">White Croats</a> that lived on the border of modern <a href="/wiki/Ukraine" title="Ukraine">Ukraine</a>. This campaign was cut short by the attacks of the <a href="/wiki/Pechenegs" title="Pechenegs">Pechenegs</a> on and around <a href="/wiki/Kyiv" title="Kyiv">Kiev</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In his later years he lived in relative peace with his other neighbors: <a href="/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_I_of_Poland" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolesław I of Poland">Bolesław I of Poland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_I_of_Hungary" title="Stephen I of Hungary">Stephen I of Hungary</a>, and Andrikh the Czech (a shadowy figure mentioned in <a href="/wiki/Primary_Chronicle" title="Primary Chronicle">A Tale of the Bygone Years</a>). After Anna's death, he married again, likely to a granddaughter of <a href="/wiki/Otto_the_Great" title="Otto the Great">Otto the Great</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 1014, his son <a href="/wiki/Yaroslav_the_Wise" title="Yaroslav the Wise">Yaroslav the Wise</a> stopped paying tribute. Vladimir decided to chastise the insolence of his son and began gathering troops against him. Vladimir fell ill, however, most likely of old age, and died at Berestove, near modern-day Kiev. The various parts of his dismembered body were distributed among his numerous sacred foundations and were venerated as <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBain1911_38-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain1911-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his Christian reign, Vladimir lived the teachings of the Bible through acts of charity. He would hand out food and drink to the less fortunate, and made an effort to go out to the people who could not reach him. His work was based on the impulse to help one's neighbors by sharing the burden of carrying their cross.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> He founded numerous churches, including the <span title="Ukrainian-language text"><i lang="uk">Desyatynna Tserkva</i></span> <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Tithes" title="Church of the Tithes">(Church, or Cathedral, of the Tithes)</a> (989), established schools, protected the poor and introduced ecclesiastical courts. He lived mostly at peace with his neighbors, the incursions of the Pechenegs alone disturbing his tranquility.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBain1911_38-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBain1911-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He introduced the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_law" title="Byzantine law">Byzantine law code</a> into his territories following his conversion but reformed some of its harsher elements; he notably abolished <a href="/wiki/Capital_punishment" title="Capital punishment">capital punishment</a>, along with <a href="/wiki/Judicial_torture" class="mw-redirect" title="Judicial torture">judicial torture</a> and <a href="/wiki/Political_mutilation_in_Byzantine_culture" title="Political mutilation in Byzantine culture">mutilation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Family_life_and_children_of_Vladimir_I" title="Family life and children of Vladimir I">Family life and children of Vladimir I</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Anton_Losenko._Vladimir_and_Rogneda.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Anton_Losenko._Vladimir_and_Rogneda.jpg/220px-Anton_Losenko._Vladimir_and_Rogneda.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="262" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Anton_Losenko._Vladimir_and_Rogneda.jpg/330px-Anton_Losenko._Vladimir_and_Rogneda.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Anton_Losenko._Vladimir_and_Rogneda.jpg/440px-Anton_Losenko._Vladimir_and_Rogneda.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1007" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption><i>Vladimir and Rogneda</i> (1770)</figcaption></figure>
<p>The fate of all Vladimir's daughters, whose number is around nine, is uncertain. His wives, concubines, and their children were as follows: 
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Olava,_Grand_Princess_of_Kiev" title="Olava, Grand Princess of Kiev">Olava</a> or Allogia (<a href="/wiki/Varangian" class="mw-redirect" title="Varangian">Varangian</a> or <a href="/wiki/Czechs" title="Czechs">Czech</a>), speculative; she might have been mother of Vysheslav while others claim that it is a confusion with <a href="/wiki/Helena_Lekapene" title="Helena Lekapene">Helena Lekapene</a><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2011)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
<ul><li>Vysheslav (c. 977&#160;&#8211;&#32;c. 1010), Prince of Novgorod (988–1010)<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Irina,_Grand_Princess_of_Kiev" title="Irina, Grand Princess of Kiev">Irina</a>, a widow of <a href="/wiki/Yaropolk_I_of_Kiev" title="Yaropolk I of Kiev">Yaropolk I</a>, a Greek nun
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Sviatopolk_I_of_Kiev" title="Sviatopolk I of Kiev">Sviatopolk the Accursed</a> (born c. 979), possibly the surviving son of Yaropolk</li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Rogneda_of_Polotsk" title="Rogneda of Polotsk">Rogneda</a> (the daughter of <a href="/wiki/Rogvolod" title="Rogvolod">Rogvolod</a>); later upon divorce she entered a convent taking the Christian name of Anastasia
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Izyaslav_of_Polotsk" class="mw-redirect" title="Izyaslav of Polotsk">Izyaslav of Polotsk</a> (born c. 979, Kiev), Prince of Polotsk (989–1001)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Yaroslav_the_Wise" title="Yaroslav the Wise">Yaroslav the Wise</a> (no earlier than 983), Prince of <a href="/wiki/Rostov" title="Rostov">Rostov</a><sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> (988–1010), Prince of Novgorod (1010–1034), Grand Prince of Kiev (1016–1018, 1019–1054). Possibly he was a son of Anna rather than Rogneda. Another interesting fact is that he was younger than Sviatopolk according to the words of <a href="/wiki/Boris_and_Gleb" title="Boris and Gleb">Boris</a> in the <i><a href="/wiki/Tale_of_Bygone_Years" class="mw-redirect" title="Tale of Bygone Years">Tale of Bygone Years</a></i> and not as it was officially known.</li>
<li>Vsevolod (c. 984&#160;&#8211;&#32;1013), possibly the Swedish Prince Wissawald of <a href="/wiki/Volhynia" title="Volhynia">Volhynia</a> (c. 1000), was perhaps the first husband of <a href="/wiki/Estrid_Svendsdatter" title="Estrid Svendsdatter">Estrid Svendsdatter</a></li>
<li>Mstislav, distinct from Mstislav of Chernigov, possibly died as an infant, if he was ever born</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mstislav_of_Chernigov" title="Mstislav of Chernigov">Mstislav of Chernigov</a> (born c. 983), Prince of <a href="/wiki/Tmutarakan" title="Tmutarakan">Tmutarakan</a> (990–1036), Prince of <a href="/wiki/Chernigov" class="mw-redirect" title="Chernigov">Chernigov</a> (1024–1036), other sources claim him to be the son of other mothers (Adela, <a href="/wiki/Malfrida" title="Malfrida">Malfrida</a>, or some other Bulgarian wife)</li>
<li>Predslava, a <a href="/wiki/Concubine" class="mw-redirect" title="Concubine">concubine</a> of <a href="/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_I_Chrobry" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolesław I Chrobry">Bolesław I Chrobry</a> according to <a href="/wiki/Gesta_principum_Polonorum" title="Gesta principum Polonorum">Gesta principum Polonorum</a></li>
<li>Premislava, (died 1015), some sources state that she was a wife of the <a href="/wiki/Ladislas_the_Bald" title="Ladislas the Bald">Duke Laszlo (Vladislav) "the Bald"</a> of the <a href="/wiki/%C3%81rp%C3%A1d_dynasty" title="Árpád dynasty">Arpadians</a></li>
<li>Mstislava, in 1018 was taken by <a href="/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_I_Chrobry" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolesław I Chrobry">Bolesław I Chrobry</a> among the other daughters</li></ul></li>
<li>Bulgarian Adela, some sources claim that Adela is not necessarily Bulgarian as Boris and Gleb may have been born from some other wife
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Boris_and_Gleb" title="Boris and Gleb">Boris</a> (born c. 986), Prince of Rostov (c. 1010&#160;&#8211;&#32;1015), remarkable is the fact that the Rostov Principality as well as the Principality of <a href="/wiki/Murom" title="Murom">Murom</a> used to border the territory of the <a href="/wiki/Volga_Bolgars" class="mw-redirect" title="Volga Bolgars">Volga Bolgars</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Boris_and_Gleb" title="Boris and Gleb">Gleb</a> (born c. 987), Prince of Murom (1013–1015), as is Boris, Gleb is also claimed to be the son of <a href="/wiki/Anna_Porphyrogenita" title="Anna Porphyrogenita">Anna Porphyrogenita</a></li>
<li>Stanislav (born c. 985&#160;&#8211;&#32;1015), Prince of Smolensk (988–1015), possibly of another wife and the fate of whom is not certain</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sudislav" title="Sudislav">Sudislav</a> (died 1063), Prince of <a href="/wiki/Pskov" title="Pskov">Pskov</a> (1014–1036), possibly of another wife, but he is mentioned in <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_Nikon" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarch Nikon">Nikon</a>'s Chronicles. He spent 35 years in prison and later became a monk.</li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Malfrida" title="Malfrida">Malfrida</a>
<ul><li>Sviatoslav (c. 982&#160;&#8211;&#32;1015), Prince of Drevlians (990–1015)</li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Anna_Porphyrogenita" title="Anna Porphyrogenita">Anna Porphyrogenita</a>
<ul><li>Theofana, a wife of Novgorod <a href="/wiki/Posadnik" title="Posadnik">posadnik</a> <a href="/wiki/Ostromir" title="Ostromir">Ostromir</a>, a grandson of semi-legendary <a href="/wiki/Dobrynya" title="Dobrynya">Dobrynya</a> (highly doubtful is the fact of her being Anna's offspring)</li></ul></li>
<li>a granddaughter of <a href="/wiki/Otto_the_Great" title="Otto the Great">Otto the Great</a> (possibly Rechlinda Otona [Regelindis])
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Maria_Dobroniega_of_Kiev" title="Maria Dobroniega of Kiev">Maria Dobroniega of Kiev</a> (born c. 1012), the Duchess of Poland (1040–1087), married around 1040 to <a href="/wiki/Casimir_I_the_Restorer" title="Casimir I the Restorer">Casimir I the Restorer</a>, Duke of Poland, her maternity as daughter of this wife is deduced from her apparent age</li></ul></li>
<li>other possible family
<ul><li>Vladimirovna, an out-of-marriage daughter (died 1044), married to <a href="/wiki/Bernard,_Margrave_of_the_Nordmark" title="Bernard, Margrave of the Nordmark">Bernard, Margrave of the Nordmark</a>.</li>
<li>Pozvizd (born prior to 988), a son of Vladimir according to <a href="/wiki/Hustyn_Chronicles" class="mw-redirect" title="Hustyn Chronicles">Hustyn Chronicles</a>. He, possibly, was the Prince Khrisokhir mentioned by <a href="/wiki/Niketas_Choniates" title="Niketas Choniates">Niketas Choniates</a>.</li></ul></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Significance_and_legacy">Significance and legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Significance and legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1_hryvnia_2006_front.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/1_hryvnia_2006_front.jpg/220px-1_hryvnia_2006_front.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="117" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/1_hryvnia_2006_front.jpg/330px-1_hryvnia_2006_front.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/1_hryvnia_2006_front.jpg/440px-1_hryvnia_2006_front.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1367" data-file-height="727" /></a><figcaption>Volodymyr the Great portrait on obverse of <a href="/wiki/Banknotes_of_the_Ukrainian_hryvnia" title="Banknotes of the Ukrainian hryvnia">₴1 bill</a>, circa 2006</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Orthodox">Eastern Orthodox</a>, <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite_Lutheranism" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Rite Lutheranism">Byzantine Rite Lutheran</a> and <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Roman Catholic</a> churches celebrate the feast day of St. Vladimir on 15/28 July.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The town <a href="/wiki/Volodymyr_(city)" title="Volodymyr (city)">Volodymyr</a> in north-western Ukraine was founded by Vladimir and is named after him.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The foundation of another town, <a href="/wiki/Vladimir,_Russia" title="Vladimir, Russia">Vladimir</a> in Russia, is usually attributed to <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Monomakh" class="mw-redirect" title="Vladimir Monomakh">Vladimir Monomakh</a>. However some researchers argue that it was also founded by Vladimir the Great.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/St_Volodymyr%27s_Cathedral" title="St Volodymyr&#39;s Cathedral">St Volodymyr's Cathedral</a>, one of the largest cathedrals in Kyiv, is dedicated to Vladimir the Great, as was originally the <a href="/wiki/Kyiv_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Kyiv University">Kyiv University</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Imperial Russian</a> <a href="/wiki/Order_of_St._Vladimir" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of St. Vladimir">Order of St. Vladimir</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Vladimir%27s_Orthodox_Theological_Seminary" title="Saint Vladimir&#39;s Orthodox Theological Seminary">Saint Vladimir's Orthodox Theological Seminary</a> in the United States are also named after him.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The memory of Vladimir was also kept alive by innumerable Russian folk ballads and legends, which refer to him as <i>Krasno Solnyshko</i> (<i>the Fair Sun</i>, or <i>the Red Sun</i>; <i>Красно Солнышко</i> in Russian). The <a href="/wiki/Varangian" class="mw-redirect" title="Varangian">Varangian</a> period of Eastern Slavic history ceases with Vladimir, and the Christian period begins.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The appropriation of Kievan Rus' as part of national history has also been a topic of contention in <a href="/wiki/Ukrainophile" class="mw-redirect" title="Ukrainophile">Ukrainophile</a> vs. <a href="/wiki/Russophilia" title="Russophilia">Russophile</a> schools of historiography since the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_historiography" class="mw-redirect" title="Soviet historiography">Soviet era</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Today, he is regarded as a symbol in Belarus, Russia and Ukraine.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>All branches of the economy prospered under Vladimir.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> He <a href="/wiki/Money_of_Kievan_Rus%27" title="Money of Kievan Rus&#39;">minted coins</a> and regulated foreign affairs with other countries, such as trade, bringing in <a href="/wiki/Greek_wine" title="Greek wine">Greek wines</a>, <a href="/wiki/Baghdad" title="Baghdad">Baghdad</a> spices, and <a href="/wiki/Arabian_horse" title="Arabian horse">Arabian horses</a> for the markets of Kiev.
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">

</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vladimir_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-9"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-9">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><i>Volodiměrъ</i> is an Old East Slavic form of the given name; this form was influenced and partially replaced by the <a href="/wiki/Old_Bulgarian" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Bulgarian">Old Bulgarian</a> (Old Church Slavonic) form <i>Vladiměrъ</i> (by folk etymology later also <i>Vladimirъ</i>; in modern East Slavic languages, the given name is rendered <a href="/wiki/Belarusian_language" title="Belarusian language">Belarusian</a>: <span lang="be">Уладзiмiр</span>, <i>Uladzimir</i>, <a href="/wiki/Russian_language" title="Russian language">Russian</a>: <span lang="ru">Владимир</span>, <i>Vladimir</i>, <a href="/wiki/Ukrainian_language" title="Ukrainian language">Ukrainian</a>: <span lang="uk">Володимир</span>, <i>Volodymyr</i>. See <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_(name)" title="Vladimir (name)">Vladimir (name)</a> for details.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-11"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-11">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Russian_language" title="Russian language">Russian</a>: <span lang="ru">Владимир Святославич</span>, <i>Vladimir Svyatoslavich</i>; <a href="/wiki/Ukrainian_language" title="Ukrainian language">Ukrainian</a>: <span lang="uk">Володимир Святославич</span>, <i>Volodymyr Sviatoslavych</i>; <a href="/wiki/Old_Norse_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Norse language">Old Norse</a> <i>Valdamarr gamli</i>;<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-31"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-31">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to historian Donald Ostrowski (2017), Russian scholars tend to prefer "Vladimir", while Ukrainian scholars tend to prefer "Volodimer". However, "Volodimir" tends to occur as much in the primary sources as "Volodimer", and significantly more often than "Vladimir".<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-46"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-46">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In 983, after another of his military successes, Prince Vladimir and his army thought it necessary to sacrifice human lives to the gods. A lot was cast and it fell on a youth, Ioann by name, the son of a Christian, Fyodor. His father stood firmly against his son being sacrificed to the idols. Further, he tried to show the pagans the futility of their faith: "Your gods are just plain wood: it is here now but it may rot into oblivion tomorrow; your gods neither eat, nor drink, nor talk and are made by human hand from wood; whereas there is only one God – He is worshiped by Greeks and He created heaven and earth; and your gods? They have created nothing, for they have been created themselves; never will I give my son to the devils!"<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2013)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
